# Accessory Navicular

*Pediatrics · 1 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — normal variant in **up to 12%** of population (Orthobullets) · **2–21%** (OB explanation) · **majority asymptomatic**

**Sex** — more commonly **symptomatic in females**

### Genes & lab markers

**Inheritance** — **autosomal dominant**

### Pathoanatomy

**Lesion** — **plantar medial enlargement** of the navicular\
✗ Wrong options: dorsal medial · dorsal lateral · plantar lateral · central

**Forms** — separate accessory bone **or** completely ossified extension of navicular

**Origin** — tuberosity develops from a **secondary ossification centre that fails to unite** in childhood · accessory navicular does **not ossify before age 8**

**Normal navicular** — single ossification centre · ossifies **age 3 (girls) / 5 (boys)** · fuses **~13 yr**

**Associated** — **flat feet (pes planus)** · **posterior tibial tendon insufficiency** (rare)

### Anatomy & tibialis posterior insertion

**Tibialis posterior** — inserts on **medial tuberosity of navicular** · innervated by **tibial nerve**\
✗ Wrong options: deep/superficial peroneal · medial/lateral plantar

**PTT function** — **inverts hindfoot + assists ankle plantarflexion**\
✗ Wrong options: dorsiflexion = tibialis anterior · plantarflexion = gastrocsoleus · eversion = peroneus brevis

![Tibialis posterior inserting on navicular tuberosity](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4070-navitp.jpg)

**Other insertions (distractors)** — FDL → bases of 2nd–5th distal phalanges · FHL → base of great-toe distal phalanx · PB → base 5th MT · PL → plantar medial cuneiform + lateral base 1st MT

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — usually **asymptomatic** · **medial arch pain**, worse with overuse/after practice · may follow a perceived **inversion injury / ankle sprain**

**Pain source** — repeated **microfracture at the synchondrosis** or **PTT insertional inflammation**

**Exam** — firm, **tender medial-plantar navicular prominence** · ± swelling · pain with **resisted inversion**

**Rigid arch supports** — rub on the prominence → **worsen pain** → use soft, cushioned inserts

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

**Radiographs** — **AP, lateral, external oblique** · **best seen on external oblique view**

**Finding** — bony enlargement or accessory ossicle at medial navicular · no periosteal reaction / destruction / matrix\
✗ Wrong options: biopsy · MRI + chest CT

**MRI** — only to evaluate **other pathology** · not needed for work-up\
✗ Wrong options: "obtain MRI" as next step

![Accessory navicular — external oblique view](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4070-obl.jpg)

### Radiographic classification

| Type | Description | Image |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Sesamoid** in substance of tibialis posterior insertion | ![Type 1](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4070-seasmoid-navicular.jpg) |
| **2** | Separate accessory bone attached via **synchondrosis** | ![Type 2](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4070-incomplete-pic-1.jpg) |
| **3** | **Complete bony enlargement** (cornuate navicular) | ![Type 3](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4070-complete-pic.jpg) |

## 5 Management

```
Symptomatic accessory navicular
├─ First line → activity + shoe modification, orthotics, NSAIDs, PT
├─ Failed above → short cast immobilization
└─ Failed extended nonop → excision through synchondrosis
       └─ Flatfoot is primary problem → add MCO + PTT reattachment
```

### Nonoperative care – orthotics & casting

**First line** — **activity restriction, shoe modification, non-narcotic analgesics/NSAIDs**, PT (✓ even after acute sprain)

**UCBL orthosis** — **inverts heel** during walking → offloads prominence → ↓ symptoms

**Orthotic rule** — must **offload** the accessory navicular or it **exacerbates** symptoms

**Cast immobilization** — **next step** after failed activity + shoe modification\
✗ Wrong options: continued activity modification · MRI · ORIF

**Outcome** — most become **asymptomatic at skeletal maturity**

### Surgery – excision ± PTT advancement; with flatfoot correction

**Indication** — recalcitrant pain after **extended nonoperative management**

**Procedure** — medial approach (distal talus → medial cuneiform) · reflect PTT / **tendon-splitting** approach · excise ossicle **through the synchondrosis** · **trim navicular body** flush with medial border of medial cuneiform

**PTT advancement (Kidner)** — Orthobullets: **do NOT advance PTT** (no added benefit, ↑ morbidity) · OB: **simple excision = Kidner** in outcomes · ✓ "split PTT and excise prominence"\
✗ Wrong options: PTT advancement · hindfoot osteotomy · osteosynthesis of ossicle (does not shrink prominence)

**Flatfoot is the primary problem** (flexible, hindfoot valgus, symptomatic Type II) — **MCO + excision + PTT reattachment** · otherwise flatfoot **not** corrected at the same time\
✗ Wrong options: isolated excision (fails) · FDL transfer · lateral closing wedge · subtalar fusion

## 6 Complications

**Most common** — **persistent medial prominence and pain** → navicular body **not trimmed enough**

## X vs Y

| | **Simple excision** | **Kidner (+ PTT advancement)** | **MCO + excision + PTT reattachment** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Use / evidence | Isolated ossicle · relieves pain · **= Kidner** | Historical · **no added benefit**, ↑ morbidity | **Flexible flatfoot + hindfoot valgus** primary |

## How it's asked

**"Accessory navicular excision — tendon elevated is innervated by?"** → **tibial nerve**

**"Function of muscle attached to the ossicle?"** → **inverts hindfoot, assists plantarflexion**

**"Rigid arch insoles make medial foot pain worse — which tendon inserts there?"** → **posterior tibialis**

**"Underlying anatomy?"** → **plantar medial enlargement of navicular**

**"Failed 6 months activity + shoe modification — next?"** → **cast immobilization**\
✗ Wrong options: MRI · continued modification

**"Symptomatic after sprain / incidental ossicle — initial treatment?"** → **activity modification, orthotics (UCBL), PT**\
✗ Wrong options: cast 6 weeks · excision + PTT advancement

**"Failed orthotics + casting — next?"** → **excise medial prominence including synchondrosis**\
✗ Wrong options: ORIF / bone stimulator for "nonunion"

**"Best surgical procedure?"** → **split PTT, excise prominence**\
✗ Wrong options: Kidner advancement (no outcome difference)

**"Symptomatic accessory navicular + flexible flatfoot with hindfoot valgus?"** → **MCO + excision + PTT reattachment**

**"Most common complication of excision?"** → **persistent prominence/pain — inadequate trimming**

# Achondroplasia

*Pediatrics · 2 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Most common skeletal dysplasia** — **80–90% of all dwarfism** (TO) \
✗ Wrong options: diastrophic · MED · nail-patella

**Incidence** — **1 in 15,000–30,000** (Orthobullets) · **1/30,000** live births (OB)

**Sex / timing** — no sex predominance · diagnosed in utero or early infancy · short stature apparent at birth

**Risk factor** — **advanced paternal age** (>50 yr: 1 in 1,875; >35 yr still ↑)

### FGFR3 genetics, inheritance & affected physeal zone (proliferative)

**Inheritance** — **sporadic 80%** (de novo) · **autosomal dominant 20%** · **100% penetrance** · once present → **AD transmission**, 50% risk per child (TO) · "most common inheritance pattern" → ✓ **AD**\
✗ Wrong options: AR · X-linked · mitochondrial

**Gene** — **FGFR3** (fibroblast growth factor **receptor** 3, not FGF-3 the ligand) on **chromosome 4p**

**Mutation** — point mutation **Gly → Arg** in **transmembrane domain** · **G380R** (OB) = nucleotide **Gly1138Arg** (Orthobullets) · always the same site\
✗ Wrong options: COL1A1 (>200 sites)

**Effect** — **gain of function** ("turned on") → ↑ **tyrosine kinase** activity → ↑ **inhibition of chondrocyte proliferation + differentiation** (TO)

**Zone** — **proliferative zone** of physis (TO) · **quantitative cartilage defect** · defect of **endochondral** ossification

**Intramembranous ossification normal** — normal skull vault, clavicles, diaphyseal **width** (TO)

**Mechanism detail** — mutation stabilises FGFR3 dimers → escapes **ubiquitin** degradation → persistent signalling

**Pathways** — **STAT1** (inhibits chondrocyte proliferation) · **MAPK** · PLCγ · PI3K-AKT

**Modulator** — **C-type natriuretic peptide (CNP)** → NPR-B → ↑ cGMP → ↓ FGFR3 activity → basis of **vosoritide**

![Physeal zones — achondroplasia = proliferative zone](../../MCQ/attachments/q220892-illa-physis.jpg)

### Genes & lab markers

| Condition | Gene / defect |
|---|---|
| **Achondroplasia** | **FGFR3** gain of function, chr 4p, ↑ tyrosine kinase |
| Hypochondroplasia | FGFR3 **Asn540Lys** |
| Thanatophoric dysplasia | FGFR3 **Lys650Glu** — lethal (respiratory) |
| SADDAN | FGFR3 **Lys650Met** |
| Apert syndrome | **FGFR2** |
| Pseudoachondroplasia | **COMP**, chr **19** |
| MED | **COMP** (chr 19) |
| SED · Kniest | **COL2A1** (type II collagen) |
| Osteogenesis imperfecta | **Type I collagen** (COL1A1 / COL1A2) |
| Diastrophic dysplasia | **SLC26A2 (DTDST)**, chr **5** — sulfate transport, **AR** |
| Cleidocranial dysplasia | **RUNX2 (CBFA1)** |
| Fibrous dysplasia | **GNAS** activating → ↑ cAMP |
| Marfan | **Fibrillin** |
| Gaucher | **Glucocerebrosidase** |
| Jansen metaphyseal chondrodysplasia | **PTHrP** |
| Schmid metaphyseal chondrodysplasia | **Type X collagen** (AD) |
| Larsen syndrome | **Filamin B** (AD form) |

### Physeal zone associations (distractors)

| Zone | Pathology |
|---|---|
| Epiphyseal bone | **MED** |
| **Reserve / resting** | **Gaucher**, **diastrophic dysplasia**, pseudoachondroplasia |
| **Proliferative** | **Achondroplasia** ✓ |
| **Hypertrophic** | **SCFE**, physeal fractures, enchondroma, rickets |
| Primary spongiosa | **Metaphyseal corner fractures** (abuse), **scurvy** |
| Secondary spongiosa | SCFE in **renal osteodystrophy** |

**Labelled-figure questions** — letter varies (B or C in different stems) → always choose the **proliferative zone**

## 3 Presentation

### Clinical features – rhizomelia, frontal bossing, trident hand

**Rhizomelic dwarfism** — **proximal** segment shortest (humerus < forearm, femur < tibia) · **disproportionate** short stature · **normal trunk** (TO)

**Height** — adult ~**50 in** (male 4′4″, female 4′) · 6–7 SD below normal

**Face** — **macrocephaly**, **frontal bossing**, **midface hypoplasia**, depressed nasal bridge

**Hands** — brachydactyly · **trident hand** = all fingers same length, divergent **middle and ring** fingers (TO)

**Upper limb** — limited elbow extension · **radial head subluxation/dislocation** · posterior humeral bowing

**Lower limb** — **genu varum** · **hypermobile hips and knees** · muscular hypotonia · long fibulae\
✗ Wrong options: genu valgum

**Spine** — **thoracolumbar kyphosis** · **lumbar hyperlordosis** (up to 80%) · **lumbar stenosis** · **foramen magnum stenosis**

**Neuro / development** — **normal intelligence** · **delayed motor milestones**

**ENT** — **otitis media 90% before age 2** · hearing loss · tonsillar hypertrophy · obesity

**NOT a feature** — **cervical spine instability** (→ pseudoachondroplasia) · **atlantoaxial instability** (→ Morquio MPS IVA, Down) · dural ectasia (→ NF, Marfan) · cauliflower ear (→ diastrophic)

![Achondroplasia clinical features](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4094-achondroplasia-clinical-features.jpg)

![Trident hand](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4094-achondroplasia-trident-hand-clinical-photograph.jpg)

### Dwarfism by segment

| Type | Segment short | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Rhizomelic** | **Proximal** (humerus, femur) | **Achondroplasia** |
| Mesomelic | Middle (forearm, leg) | **Leri-Weill dyschondrosteosis** |
| Mesoacromelic | Middle + distal | **Ellis–van Creveld** |
| Acromelic | Distal (hands, feet) | **Peripheral dysostosis** |

| Category (TO) | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Short trunk** | Kniest, metatrophic dysplasia, **SED** |
| Proportionate | Diastrophic, cleidocranial dysplasia, mucopolysaccharidoses |
| **Disproportionate (short limb)** | **Achondroplasia**, metaphyseal chondrodysplasias |

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

**Diagnosis** — clinical + **skeletal survey** in most · **FGFR3 molecular testing** only if atypical / to distinguish similar disorders

**Prenatal** — US 2nd/3rd trimester: short long bones, wide metaphyses, **↑ biparietal diameter (>95th centile)** · cell-free fetal DNA

**Sleep study** — **all infants** (screen for apnea)

### Key radiology findings

**Views** — **skeletal survey** · **AP pelvis + femur** (most diagnostic features)

**Spine** — **↓ interpedicular distance L1 → S1/L5 (pathognomonic)** · **short pedicles** · posterior vertebral scalloping · T12/L1 apical wedging · bullet-shaped vertebrae in infants · hyperlordosis with flat sacrum

**Pelvis** — **champagne-glass pelvis** (wider than deep) · **squared ("ping-pong paddle") iliac wings** · **narrow sciatic notch** · flat horizontal acetabula

**Limbs** — short thick bones · metaphyseal flaring · **inverted-V distal femoral physis** · proximal femoral radiolucency · **epiphyses normal**

**MRI** — FMS: obliterated posterior subarachnoid space, cord "nicking/waisting", T2 signal change, syrinx · also lumbar stenosis

![Champagne-glass pelvis](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4094-shows-a-quot-champagne-glass-quot-pelvis.jpg)

![Short pedicles, posterior scalloping](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4094-xray-lateral-spine-colorado-moved.jpg)

## 5 Management

### Thoracolumbar kyphosis – natural history

**Most common dysplasia with TLK** — **achondroplasia**\
✗ Wrong options: Trevor · MED (normal spine) · Larsen (cervical kyphosis) · diastrophic (cervical)

**Cause** — flexible, forward slouching with unsupported sitting · 50–100% born with TLK

**Natural history** — **resolves by 12–18 months** (OB) · **1.5–2 years** (TO) as child **walks** and gains trunk strength · resolution 90% by 18 months · ~70% by age 3 (OB) · prevalence ~87% at 1–2 yr → ~10% at 5–10 yr

```
Thoracolumbar kyphosis in achondroplasia
├─ Flexible (most) → observe; no unsupported sitting
├─ Fixed >30° / anterior wedging → TLSO until walking
└─ Neuro deficit / failed brace / >45–60° (>50° TO) → posterior fusion ± anterior decompression
       (delay to age ≥4 for instrumentation)
```

**Observation** — firm-backed seating · avoid C-position · **no sitting >60°**

**Bracing (TLSO)** — **fixed component >30°** (prone lateral) · substantial anterior wedging · posterior apical displacement · worn until independent walking · mean residual 8°

**Fusion** — neuro compromise · kyphosis **>45–60°** (Orthobullets) · fixed **>50°** (TO) · failed brace · **wait until age 4** · corpectomy if severe anterior cord impingement

### Foramen magnum stenosis – sleep apnea

**FMS → central sleep apnea** → **SIDS** (2–5% mortality in 1st year) (TO)

**Central vs obstructive** — **adenotonsillar hypertrophy → obstructive** apnea, no hypotonia · **FMS → central** apnea + **hypotonia**

**Signs** — clonus, **hypotonia**, hyperreflexia, clumsiness, **dysphagia** (lower CN), weakness

**Work-up** — **sleep study all infants** · **MRI/CT** if abnormal exam or sleep study

```
Foramen magnum stenosis
├─ No central apnea, no compression, neuro intact → observe, repeat MRI 6–12 mo
└─ Central apnea / cord compression / neuro deficit → foramen magnum decompression + upper cervical laminectomy ± duraplasty
       (intra-op US; 10% recurrence)
```

### Lumbar stenosis

**Prevalence** — 37–89% · **25% need surgery** · disability in **3rd–4th decade**

**Causes** — short thick pedicles · ↓ interpedicular distance L1→L5 · hyperplastic disc + ligamentum flavum

**First line** — weight loss, PT, corticosteroid injections

**Surgery** — severe neurogenic claudication · failed nonop · neuro symptoms at rest → **multilevel laminectomy + fusion**

**Technique** — **3 levels above block → at least S2** · **no sublaminar wires / laminar hooks** (narrow canal) · pedicle screws preferred but pedicles **cranially directed, ~10 mm shorter**, smaller sagittal diameter

**Post-laminectomy kyphosis** — **common** despite >50% facet preservation → **fuse if ≥5 levels or skeletally immature**

### Operative criteria

| Problem | Surgery when |
|---|---|
| **TLK — brace** | Fixed **>30°**, anterior wedging |
| **TLK — fusion** | Neuro deficit · **>45–60°** (Orthobullets) / **>50°** (TO) · failed brace · age ≥4 |
| **FMS** | **Central sleep apnea** · cord compression · weakness, hyperreflexia, clonus |
| **Lumbar stenosis** | Severe claudication · failed nonop · neuro symptoms at rest |
| **Genu varum** | Progressive severe deformity · **fibular thrust** |
| **Upper-limb lengthening** | **Bilateral** humeral shortening preventing **perineal / head hygiene** (TO) |

### Extremities & stature

**Genu varum** — bracing generally unsuccessful · **tibial ± femoral osteotomy** at CORA for progressive deformity / fibular thrust · TO: osteotomy > growth modulation

**Growth hormone** — controversial · +6–8 cm over 5 yr

**Vosoritide** — **CNP analogue** · daily injection · age **≥5 yr with open physes**

**Lower-limb lengthening** — very controversial · early adolescence or two stages (age 7 + 12) · gain ~20.5 cm

### Upper-limb lengthening

**Indication** — **bilateral affection** with humeral shortening limiting **perineal hygiene** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: unilateral LLD (upper limb non-weight-bearing, cosmetic)

**Gain** — humerus ~10 cm

**Complications** — **25–43%**: fracture, early consolidation, nonunion, malalignment, stiffness, infection (TO)

## 6 Complications

**Hydrocephalus** — shunt in **5%**

**ENT** — otitis media 90% < age 2 · **50% need tympanostomy tubes**

**Spinal surgery** — recurrent FMS · CSF leak · malpositioned screw (abnormal pedicles) · neuro injury (overcorrection, wires/hooks) · **post-laminectomy kyphosis**

**Life expectancy** — ~**61 yr** (10 yr less) · highest mortality **<4 yr** (FMS)

## X vs Y

| | **Achondroplasia** | **Pseudoachondroplasia** | **Hypochondroplasia** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gene | **FGFR3** (chr 4p) | **COMP** (chr 19) | FGFR3 (Asn540Lys) |
| Face | **Frontal bossing**, midface hypoplasia | **Normal face/skull** | Milder |
| Onset | **At birth** | Normal at birth, diagnosed **2–3 yr** | Often **puberty** (no growth spurt) |
| Spine | **Spinal stenosis**, ↓ interpedicular distance | **No stenosis** (normal interpedicular distance); **C1–C2 instability** (odontoid hypoplasia) | Craniocervical issues less common |
| Epiphyses | **Normal** | **Epiphysis + metaphysis** involved, early hip dysplasia | Similar X-ray to achondroplasia |
| Other | Normal IQ | — | Taller; seizures/cognitive issues more common |

| | **FMS** | **Adenotonsillar hypertrophy** |
|---|---|---|
| Apnea | **Central** | **Obstructive** |
| Hypotonia | **Yes** | No |
| Test | Sleep study → **MRI/CT** | Sleep study |

## How it's asked

**"Achondroplasia — which zone of the physis?"** → **zone of proliferation**\
✗ Wrong options: hypertrophy · resting · provisional calcification

**"Receptor defective and zone affected?"** → **FGFR3, proliferative zone**\
✗ Wrong options: FGFR2 = Apert · COMP

**"FGFR3 mutation is associated with?"** → **achondroplasia**\
✗ Wrong options: Marfan · Gaucher · fibrous dysplasia · diastrophic

**"G380R transmembrane FGFR3 — which radiograph?"** → **achondroplasia**\
✗ Wrong options: rickets cupping · osteopetrosis · OI · cleidocranial

**"Mother and daughter, short stature, frontal bossing — gene?"** → **FGFR3**\
✗ Wrong options: COMP chr 19 · RUNX2/CBFA1

**"AD, chr 4p, ↑ tyrosine kinase in proliferative zone?"** → **achondroplasia**\
✗ Wrong options: pseudoachondroplasia · Larsen · diastrophic · Kniest

**"Defect in rhizomelic dwarf with champagne pelvis?"** → **FGFR3**\
✗ Wrong options: type I/II collagen · COMP · sulfate transport

**"Achondroplasia caused by abnormality of?"** → **FGFR3**\
✗ Wrong options: PTHrP = Jansen · BMP · TGF-β · IGF-BP

**"Genetic defect?"** → **FGFR3 receptor**\
✗ Wrong options: FGF-3 · COL1A1 · trisomy 21

**"Most common inheritance pattern?"** → **autosomal dominant** (even though 80% sporadic)

**"Pedigree consistent with rhizomelic dwarfism?"** → **autosomal dominant pedigree**

**"Abnormal tyrosine phosphorylation + FMS in toddler?"** → **gain-of-function FGFR3**\
✗ Wrong options: COMP = MED · COL2A1 = SED · PTHrP = Jansen · type X = Schmid

**"Rhizomelic dwarfism refers to?"** → **short proximal limb segments**

**"FGFR3 dwarfism — all EXCEPT?"** → **cervical spine instability** (pseudoachondroplasia)

**"Extremity radiographs in G380R child?"** → **disproportionate, proximal segment short**\
✗ Wrong options: proportionate · mesomelic · acromelic

**"Most common skeletal dysplasia?"** → **achondroplasia**

**"9-month-old with back deformity, champagne pelvis — management?"** → **resolves by 1.5–2 years** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: laminectomy + fusion · growing rods

**"Natural history of infant TLK?"** → **spontaneous resolution by 12–18 months**

**"Most common dysplasia with thoracolumbar kyphosis?"** → **achondroplasia**

**"Associated with achondroplasia?"** → **(central) sleep apnea** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: genu valgum · LLD · atlantoaxial instability · dural ectasia · cauliflower ear

**"Hypotonic 3-year-old with central apnea — work-up for?"** → **foramen magnum stenosis**\
✗ Wrong options: C1–C2 instability · adenotonsillar hypertrophy · tethered cord

**"Indication for upper-limb lengthening?"** → **bilateral affection** (perineal hygiene) (TO)

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Adolescent Blount's Disease

*Pediatrics · 3 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Definition & epidemiology

**Definition** — progressive pathologic **genu varum** centred at proximal tibia, onset **>10 years** · idiopathic proximal tibia vara

**Risk factors** — **obesity** (causative) · **African-American** descent · more common in males (OB)

**Associated** — **hypertension** (often undiagnosed, under-treated, modifiable) · vitamin D deficiency prevalence = healthy children\
✗ Wrong options: rickets

**Onset vs diagnosis** — deformity often begins 4–10 yr · diagnosed as adolescent form **after age 10** (OB)

### Pathophysiology

**Cause** — **dyschondrosis of medial proximal tibial physis** (posteromedial) · **mechanical overload** in **genetically susceptible** child

**Deformity** — genu varum + procurvatum + internal tibial torsion

**Associated deformity** — **distal femoral varus** + **distal tibial valgus** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: compensatory distal femoral valgus (that's infantile)

## 3 Presentation

**Hallmark** — **genu varum**, usually **unilateral** · obesity · "waddle"

**Other** — LLD secondary to deformity · mild–moderate **MCL laxity** · medial knee pain

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Diagnosis – obesity & late-onset tibia vara

**Diagnosis** — clinical genu varum + radiographic **↑ metaphyseal–diaphyseal angle**

**Differentials ruled out** — congenital hypothyroidism (presents earlier) · metabolic syndrome (normal A1c, TGs) · primary varus gonarthrosis (adult) · vitamin D deficiency/rickets (**bilateral** femoral + tibial bowing)

**Physiologic** — genu varum normal **<2 years**

### Key radiology findings

**Views** — **standing long-cassette AP both lower limbs**, **patellae forward** (internal tibial torsion common)

**Findings** — narrowing of tibial epiphysis · **widening of medial tibial physis** · occasional widening of lateral distal femoral physis · **metaphyseal beaking less common** than infantile

**Metaphyseal–diaphyseal (Drennan) angle** — line through metaphyseal beaks vs perpendicular to tibial axis · **>16° abnormal**

**Tibiofemoral angle** — femoral vs tibial longitudinal axes

![Metaphyseal–diaphyseal (Drennan) angle](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4051-metaphyseal-diaphyseal-angle-moved.jpg)

**Classification** — **no radiographic classification** for adolescent form (Langenskiöld = infantile) · severity graded mild / moderate / severe by **mechanical axis deviation (MAD)**

## 5 Management

**Rule** — **surgery** (TO) · observation/bracing **unlikely to work** — progressive, never resolves spontaneously · untreated → medial pain, altered kinematics, **early arthritis**\
✗ Wrong options: orthotics

```
Adolescent Blount
├─ Mild–moderate, growth remaining (>2 yr) → lateral tibial ± fibular hemiepiphysiodesis
└─ Severe / near or at maturity (<2 yr growth) → proximal tibial osteotomy
       ├─ No LLD → focal dome osteotomy at CORA
       ├─ LLD corrected by opening wedge → medial opening wedge HTO + plate
       └─ LLD too large → osteotomy + external fixator (gradual)
```

**Distal femoral varus ≥8°** → **distal femoral osteotomy or epiphysiodesis**

### Operative criteria

| Situation | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Mild–moderate + significant growth remaining | **Lateral tibia + fibula hemiepiphysiodesis** (8-plate / staple / permanent) |
| Closed physes / insufficient growth (e.g. 15 yr ♀, triradiate fused) | **Osteotomy** (✗ Wrong options: growth modulation) |
| Varus + LLD 2 cm, physes closed | **Medial opening wedge HTO + plate** |
| LLD not correctable by opening wedge | Osteotomy + **external fixator** |
| Distal femoral varus **≥8°** | Distal femoral osteotomy / epiphysiodesis |
| Goal | **Neutral mechanical axis** (✗ Wrong options: overcorrection into valgus (that's infantile)) |

### Treatment – hemiepiphysiodesis (failure) & osteotomy

**Principle** — **Hueter-Volkmann law**: lateral tether slows lateral growth → medial "catches up"\
✗ Wrong options: flexible nails = secondary healing · VDRO · Ilizarov distraction · lag screw = primary healing

**Transient hemiepiphysiodesis** — **8-plate or staple** · removable · needs significant growth · watch for physeal arrest or **rebound** growth

**Permanent hemiepiphysiodesis** — lateral physeal ablation · overcorrection uncommon · no rotational correction · does not preclude later osteotomy

**Residual deformity** — up to **25%** need formal osteotomy

**Acute HTO + internal fixation** — closing, opening, dome, serrated, inclined osteotomies · immediate correction · risks **neurologic injury** (acute lengthening) + **compartment syndrome** → consider **prophylactic fasciotomy** · PWB 6–8 wk

**Gradual correction (Taylor Spatial Frame / Ilizarov)** — 12–18 wk · **limits NV compromise + compartment syndrome** · all planes + length · cons: pin-site infection, duration, bulk

![Taylor spatial frame for adolescent Blount](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4051-taylor-spatial-frame-for-adolescent-blount-39-s.jpg)

### Paley osteotomy rules (tibia vara)

| Rule | Osteotomy vs ACA/CORA | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | Osteotomy **through ACA-CORA** | Pure angulation, **no translation** |
| **2** | ACA through CORA, osteotomy elsewhere | Angulation + **bone-end translation**, axes **collinear** |
| **3** | Osteotomy through ACA, CORA at another level | No bone-end translation, axes **parallel but translated** |

**Best osteotomy** — **focal dome centred on CORA** → restores alignment, minimal axis translation + shortening, least bone-end translation, most bone contact\
✗ Wrong options: intra-articular (chondral/cruciate risk) · horizontal (translates ends + axes)

## 6 Complications

**Most common complication of growth modulation** — **implant failure** (screw breakage) (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: recurrence (infantile / genu valgum) · complete physeal arrest (not seen) · failure of correction (severe physeal disease, rare)

**Commonest failure site** — **tibial metaphyseal screw**

| Hardware-failure risk factor | ✓ ↑ risk | ✗ distractor |
|---|---|---|
| Deformity | **Greater severity** | — |
| Onset | **Earlier** (younger) | Later onset |
| Screws | **Cannulated** | Solid screws |
| BMI | **Higher** | Lower BMI |
| Metal | **Titanium** → use **stainless steel** | — |
| Construct | Staple = screw-plate (equal) | Staple constructs |

**Other** — undercorrection (too little growth) · **rebound deformity** · NV injury, compartment syndrome (acute osteotomy) · pin-site infection (frame)

## X vs Y

| | **Infantile Blount** | **Adolescent Blount** |
|---|---|---|
| Age | **2–5 yr** | **>10 yr** |
| Frequency | More common | Less common |
| Side | **~50% bilateral** | **Usually unilateral** |
| Risks | Early walking, large stature, obesity | **Obesity** |
| Classification | **Langenskiöld** | **None** |
| Location | Physeal / epiphyseal | Metaphyseal |
| Severity | More severe physeal change | Milder |
| Femur | **Compensatory distal femoral valgus** | **Distal femoral varus** (+ distal tibial valgus) |
| Natural history | Stage II–IV may resolve (before 4 yr) | **Progressive, never resolves** |
| Treatment | **Bracing** + surgery | **Surgery only** |
| Osteotomy goal | Overcorrect into valgus | **Neutral axis** |

## How it's asked

**"Feature of adolescent Blount?"** → **associated distal femoral varus** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: bilaterality · self-resolution · bracing first line

**"Obese hypertensive 10-year-old with unilateral bowing?"** → **adolescent Blount**\
✗ Wrong options: metabolic syndrome · vitamin D deficiency

**"15-year-old, fused triradiate, varus + 2 cm LLD?"** → **medial opening wedge osteotomy** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: hemiepiphysiodesis · bracing · lateral closing wedge

**"Closed physes, varus + 2 cm LLD?"** → **medial opening wedge HTO + plate**\
✗ Wrong options: medial closing wedge + ex-fix (worsens varus + LLD)

**"Most common complication of growth modulation?"** → **implant failure** (TO)

**"Osteotomy with least translation, most contact?"** → **focal dome at CORA**

**"Which procedure uses the Hueter-Volkmann law?"** → **hemiepiphysiodesis**

**"Broken 8-plate at 4 weeks — contributor?"** → **increased deformity severity**\
✗ Wrong options: later onset · solid screws · lower BMI · staples

**"Broken titanium plate screws — what may have prevented it?"** → **stainless steel implants**\
✗ Wrong options: cannulated screws · waiting for maturity

**"Why gradual frame correction?"** → **↓ neurovascular injury + compartment syndrome**

# Ankle Fractures - Pediatric

*Pediatrics · 4 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **25–40% of all physeal injuries** (**2nd most common**) · 5% of pediatric fractures

**Demographics** — males **2:1** · age **8–15 yr**

**Risk factors** — sports · ↑ BMI

**Patterns** — SH I, II · **SH III → Tillaux**, medial malleolus · **SH IV → triplane**, medial malleolus

### Anatomy – physes

**Distal tibial physis** — **35–40% of tibial growth**, 15–20% of limb growth · **3–4 mm/yr** · growth until **14 yr girls / 16 yr boys** · closes over **18 months**

**Closure order** — **central → anteromedial → posteromedial → lateral** (basis of Tillaux, triplane)

![Distal tibial physeal closure: central → anteromedial → posteromedial → lateral](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4027-pediatric-ankle-fractures.jpg)

**Distal fibular physis** — closes **12–24 months after** distal tibia

**Ligaments** — origins **distal to physes** → physis fails first

| Ligament | Attachments | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **AITFL** | **Chaput** (anterolateral tibial epiphysis) → **Wagstaffe** (anterior fibula) | Avulses **Tillaux** / transitional fractures |
| PITFL | **Volkmann** tubercle → posterior fibula | — |
| Inferior transverse | Posterior fibula across posterior tibial plafond | Posterior labrum |
| Deep deltoid | — | Primary restraint to lateral talar shift |

## 2 Mechanism

**Mechanism** — direct trauma · **twisting about a planted foot**

**High-energy abduction injuries** (e.g. skateboarding) → ↑ physeal closure risk

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — pain, inability to bear weight

**Exam** — swelling, ecchymosis, deformity if displaced · **distal fibular physeal tenderness = nondisplaced SH I**

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

**Views** — **AP, mortise, lateral** · full-length tibia to rule out **Maisonneuve**

**CT** — displacement (**best post-reduction**), articular step-off, pre-op planning

**Physeal widening** after reduction → **interposed periosteum**

### Salter-Harris classification

| Type | Freq. | Description | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | 15% | Through physis | Nondisplaced: fibular tenderness only |
| **II** | **45%** | Physis + metaphysis (**Thurston-Holland**) | Most common |
| **III** | 25% | Physis + epiphysis | **Tillaux**, medial malleolus · ↑ arrest |
| **IV** | 25% | Metaphysis + physis + epiphysis | **Triplane**, medial malleolus shear · ↑ arrest |
| **V** | 1% | Crush | Diagnosed late at growth arrest |
| **VI** | Rare | Perichondral ring | Lawnmower / iatrogenic |

![Salter-Harris classification](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4027-illustration-of-the-salter-harris-fracture-classification.jpg)

### Dias & Tachdjian (mechanism) classification

| Mechanism | Injury |
|---|---|
| **Supination–inversion** | Gr 1: **fibular SH I/II** avulsion · Gr 2: + **medial malleolus SH III/IV** |
| **Supination–plantarflexion** | Tibial SH I/II, epiphysis displaced **posteriorly**, posterior Thurston-Holland · **no fibular fracture** · hard to see on AP |
| **Supination–external rotation** | Gr 1: tibial SH II, fragment **posteromedial**, line runs proximal-medial on AP · Gr 2: + **low spiral fibula** |
| **Pronation/eversion–ER** | Tibial SH I/II + **transverse fibula**, displaced **laterally** · ± diastasis |
| **Axial compression** | **SH V** — diagnosed late |

## 5 Management

### Operative criteria

| Fracture | Nonop | CRPP | ORIF |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Isolated distal fibula** | **<2 mm** → boot / NWB SLC 4 wk · >2 mm acceptable CR → NWB cast 6 wk | >2 mm, failed CR, **>2 yr growth** | >2 mm, failed CR, **<2 yr growth** |
| **Distal tibia SH I/II** | Acceptable CR → NWB cast 6 wk | Unacceptable CR + **>2 yr growth** | Unacceptable CR + **<2 yr growth** |
| **Distal tibia SH III/IV** | — | **<2 mm** after CR | **>2 mm** after CR |

**Acceptable SH I/II tibia** — **no varus · <10° valgus · <10° recurvatum/procurvatum · <3 mm physeal widening**

**Acceptable tibial reduction** — **<2 mm** · confirm on **mortise**

```
Displaced distal tibial physeal fracture
├─ CR acceptable → long-leg NWB cast 6 wk
└─ Irreducible / residual physeal gap → open reduction, remove interposed periosteum
       ├─ Stable after open reduction → cast
       └─ Unstable → internal fixation (smooth wires across physis)
```

### Irreducible fractures – interposed periosteum & open reduction

**Closed reduction** — adequate sedation + relaxation · **max 2 attempts** (repeated attempts injure physis)

**Block to reduction** — **periosteum on the tension side** · also tendon, NV structures\
✗ Wrong options: compression side · comminution · unrecognised fracture line

![Periosteum entrapped on the tension side](../../MCQ/attachments/q210245-illa-entrapped-periosteum.jpg)

**Failed CR under sedation** → **ORIF**\
✗ Wrong options: repeat CR under GA · cast and follow-up · admit

**Why open** — interposed periosteum → **premature physeal closure** (makes bone, cartilage or fibrous tissue in physis) \
✗ Wrong options: "to prevent nonunion" (nonunion rare)

**SH I lower extremity** — growth problems are **common**\
✗ Wrong options: "rare"

### Salter-Harris III/IV medial malleolus – ORIF parallel to physis

**SH III/IV medial malleolus** — large **cartilaginous** portion (bigger than X-ray fragment) → articular incongruity → **ORIF avoiding physis and joint**\
✗ Wrong options: casting · CAM walker

**Fixation** — **transepiphyseal, parallel to physis** · 2 parallel epiphyseal screws (medial malleolus shear) · cannulated screws parallel to physis (Tillaux, triplane) · transverse metaphyseal screw if large fragment · **smooth K-wires** if crossing physis\
✗ Wrong options: screw crossing physis

**Follow-up** — long-term (high growth-arrest rate)

## 6 Complications

### Physeal arrest risk – residual gap

**Rate** — premature physeal closure **8–50%** after distal tibial physeal fractures

**Highest growth disturbance** — **medial malleolus SH IV**

| Risk factor for premature physeal closure | |
|---|---|
| **Residual physeal gap** — **greatest risk** | **>3 mm** (Orthobullets, OB) · **>2 mm** (OB) — read the options |
| Initial displacement | **+15% per 1 mm** |
| Entrapped periosteum | Yes |
| High-energy (abduction, skateboarding) | Yes (✗ Wrong options: football) |
| **SH III / IV** | Yes (all types at risk) |
| **Age >8 yr** | **NOT a risk factor** |
| Number of reductions, ORIF vs closed | not associated if anatomic |

**Partial arrest** — **distal fibular arrest → valgus** · **medial tibial arrest → varus** (continued fibular growth)

**Complete arrest** — **LLD**

### Physeal bar – resection, osteotomy & fibular epiphysiodesis

```
Distal tibial physeal bar
├─ <50% area + >2 yr growth → bar resection + interposition (fat)
│      (add osteotomy if angulation >10–20°)
├─ >50% + ≥2 yr growth → complete epiphysiodesis + ipsilateral fibular epiphysiodesis
└─ Skeletally mature with deformity → osteotomy
```

**Bar resection** — **<50% physis**, **>2 yr (or 2 cm) growth**, angulation **<20°** (Orthobullets) · interpose fat, cranioplast, bone wax, cartilage, muscle, silicone · via **metaphyseal window**\
✗ Wrong options: through the joint (bar not visible)

**Osteotomy** — angulation **>10–20°** (won't remodel after resection) → **osteotomy + bar resection through osteotomy site**

**Fibular epiphysiodesis** — bar **>50%** + ≥2 yr growth + open fibular physis → prevents **varus** + lateral impingement

**Bar types** — **peripheral** (angular deformity, resectable) · **elongated** (SH III/IV, middle) · **central** (tents articular surface) · small **<25% central** = best prognosis

**LLD** — bar resection if <50% + >2 yr growth · **contralateral epiphysiodesis** near maturity

### Rotational malunion

**Unrecognised deformity** — **external rotation** through physis, noticed months after walking (↑ foot-progression angle)\
✗ Wrong options: internal rotation · procurvatum/recurvatum (seen and corrected initially) · valgus

**Prevention / treatment** — correct rotation at casting · **derotational osteotomy**

**Other malunions** — anterior angulation / plantarflexion after **supination–plantarflexion SH II** · **valgus** after **ER SH II**

### Others

**Arthritis** — articular step-off **>2 mm**

**Extensor retinacular syndrome** — posteriorly displaced fractures

**Reflex sympathetic dystrophy** — more common in **girls** · PT, counselling, drugs, sympathetic block

## X vs Y

| | **Distal fibular arrest** | **Medial distal tibial arrest** |
|---|---|---|
| Deformity | **Valgus** | **Varus** |
| Prevention | — | **Fibular epiphysiodesis** (bar >50%) |

| Scenario | Fibular epiphysiodesis? |
|---|---|
| 13 yr ♂, **>50% bar**, 5 months post-fracture | **✓ Yes** |
| 10 yr ♀, <50% bar, ≥2 yr growth | ✗ Wrong option: bar resection |
| 13 yr ♀, <50% central bar, <2 yr growth | ✗ Wrong option: nothing (little growth) |
| 15 yr ♀, 25° varus 2 yr later | ✗ Wrong option: osteotomy (mature) |
| 10 yr ♂, SH IV with >3 mm residual | ✗ Wrong option: repeat reduction |

## How it's asked

**"Supination-inversion fracture, CR under sedation unchanged — next?"** → **ORIF**\
✗ Wrong options: CR under GA · cast

**"Physeal widening after two CR attempts?"** → **open reduction, remove periosteum, smooth wires — to ↓ premature physeal closure**\
✗ Wrong options: to prevent nonunion

**"Block to reduction?"** → **periosteum on the tension side**

**"NOT a risk factor for premature physeal closure?"** → **age >8 years**

**"Greatest risk of premature physeal closure?"** → **residual gap after CR**\
✗ Wrong options: initial displacement · attempts · ORIF

**"6-year-old SH IV medial malleolus?"** → **ORIF parallel to physis**\
✗ Wrong options: screw crossing physis

**"12-year-old SH III/IV medial malleolus?"** → **ORIF avoiding physis and joint**

**"SH IV 2 yr ago, 30° varus, small central bar?"** → **osteotomy + bar resection + fat through osteotomy**\
✗ Wrong options: resection alone · arthroscopic through joint · epiphysiodesis

**"Fibular epiphysiodesis indicated when?"** → **>50% distal tibial bar with ≥2 yr growth**

**"Deformity unrecognised until months after walking?"** → **external rotation**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Anterolateral Bowing & Congenital Pseudoarthrosis of Tibia

*Pediatrics · 5 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — extremely rare · **1:140,000–190,000** (Orthobullets) · **1 in 250,000** live births (TO)

**Continuum** — **anterolateral bowing → congenital pseudarthrosis of tibia (CPT)** = same disease\
✗ Wrong options: calcaneovalgus · fibular hemimelia · CTEV · CVT

**Pathophysiology** — pseudarthrosis is **not congenital** · develops **post-natally** from fracture nonunion

### NF1 association & natural history (fracture/pseudarthrosis)

**NF1** — **most common association** · in **50–55%** of anterolateral bowing/CPT (Orthobullets, TO) · 50–90% in large series (TO)

**Reverse** — only **6–10% of NF1** patients have anterolateral bowing (TO: **~6%**)\
✗ Wrong options: 20% · 50% · NF2

**Fibrous dysplasia** — **15%**

**First sign** — presents in **1st year** → may be **first recognised manifestation of NF1** · NF does **not** change union rate or outcome (TO)

**NF1 genetics** — **neurofibromin**, **chromosome 17**, usually **spontaneous** mutation · café-au-lait spots

**NF1 orthopaedic manifestations** — **scoliosis** (dystrophic + non-dystrophic), **CPT**, soft-tissue tumours · **nerve sheath tumours** (neurofibromas) → "not associated with NF1?" → **none of the above** (TO)

**Natural history** — does **not** self-resolve · **spontaneous fracture** at site of bowing → nonunion · once fractured, low chance of spontaneous healing\
✗ Wrong options: resolution by 2 yr · no change · spread

### Three types of tibial bowing

| Bowing | Cause | Natural history |
|---|---|---|
| **Anterolateral** | **NF1 / CPT** | **Fracture → pseudarthrosis**; no remodelling |
| **Posteromedial** | **Physiologic** (intrauterine packing) · **calcaneovalgus** foot | Resolves over **5–7 yr** · **LLD 3–4 cm** · rarely fractures |
| **Anteromedial** | **Fibular hemimelia (deficiency)** | — |
| Posterolateral | No specific association | — |

## 3 Presentation

**Onset** — bowing in **first year** of life

**Exam** — anterolateral deformity · **café-au-lait spots** + other NF stigmata

**Fibula** — dysplastic fibula / fibular pseudarthrosis in **two-thirds** (TO) · **one-third** (other TO explanation) — read the options

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

**Radiographs** — anterolateral bow · **intramedullary sclerosis / narrowed or obliterated canal** at apex (**prefracture stage**) · cystic change · hourglass constriction · tapered, sclerotic ends

![Anterolateral bowing of tibia](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4056-tibial-bowing-xray-courtesy-miller-moved.png)

![Congenital pseudarthrosis of tibia](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4056-congenital-pseudo-xray-courtesy-miller-moved.png)

### Treatment-guiding criteria

**Classifications** — Boyd, Andersen, Crawford: **none guide management or predict outcome**

**Two practical criteria** — **fracture present or absent** · **age at fracture: early onset <4 yr, late onset >4 yr**

### Boyd classification (TO)

| Type | Feature | Prognosis |
|---|---|---|
| I | Anterior bowing + tibial **defect at birth** | — |
| **II** | Anterior bowing + **hourglass constriction**; fracture **<2 yr** ("high-risk tibia"), sclerotic, canal obliterated | **Most common**, **NF-associated**, **poorest** |
| III | **Congenital cyst** (middle/distal third junction) | Recurrence less than II |
| IV | **Sclerotic segment**, no narrowing; stress fracture | **Good** |
| V | **Dysplastic fibula** | Good if confined to fibula |
| VI | Intraosseous **neurofibroma / schwannoma** | Extremely rare |

### Crawford classification (TO) — all anterolateral

| Type | Feature | Prognosis |
|---|---|---|
| I | **Preserved canal**, cortical thickening at apex | **Good**, may never fracture |
| II | Thinned canal, cortical thickening, trabeculation defect | — |
| III | **Cystic** lesion | **Early fracture** → early treatment |
| IV | Established pseudarthrosis (tibia ± fibula) | — |

**Andersen** — dysplastic, cystic, sclerotic, clubfoot types · **El-Rosasy–Paley–Herzenberg** — I atrophic mobile, no surgery · II atrophic mobile, previous surgery · III broad ends, stiff · **Choi fibula** — A (fibula intact) · B (fibular pseudarthrosis, B1–B3)

## 5 Management

```
Anterolateral bowing / CPT
├─ Bowing, no fracture, ambulatory → brace (clamshell / PTB / AFO anterior shell) to maturity
│      (TO: 8-plate guided growth for cystic, unfractured tibia)
├─ Fracture or pseudarthrosis → resect, realign, graft, IM rod ± frame / vascularised fibula / 4-in-1
└─ Multiple failed unions, severe LLD → Syme / Boyd amputation
```

### Bracing to prevent fracture

**Indication** — ambulatory (weight-bearing) child with **bowing, no fracture/pseudarthrosis**

**Brace** — **clamshell** orthosis · **patellar tendon bearing (PTB)** orthosis · **AFO with anterior shell** · until **skeletal maturity**

**Goal** — **prevent further bowing + fracture**\
✗ Wrong options: correct deformity (bowing is not brace-correctable)

**Contraindicated** — **osteotomy for bowing alone** (→ nonunion)\
✗ Wrong options: observation · IM rod · vascularised fibula · PT

**Posteromedial bowing case** — "pseudarthrosis, brace" is the **false** statement · observation only, LLD, calcaneovalgus association (TO)

### Growth modulation for deformity

**Unfractured anterolateral bowing with cystic change** — **distal tibial anterolateral 8-plate (guided growth)** → gradual cyst resolution + correction (TO, Laine 2020)\
✗ Wrong options: osteotomy + IM nail/plate (nonunion) · 4-in-1 (for established pseudarthrosis)

### Surgical indications & NF1 association

**Surgical indication** — **pseudarthrosis or fracture** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: bowing >45° · failure of brace to correct

**Goals** — resect pseudarthrosis to normal bone · correct alignment · bone graft + stabilise · **intramedullary splinting**

| Technique | Key point |
|---|---|
| **IM rod + bone graft** | Resect, **shorten tibia**, rod, graft |
| **Free vascularised fibula (Farmer)** | **Contralateral** fibula (ipsilateral involved) · when IM nailing + graft fails |
| **Ilizarov / circular frame** | Lengthening or bone transport |
| **4-in-1 (Choi)** | Resect both sclerotic ends + cortical onlay grafts → **tibiofibular cross-union** · for Choi **B2/B3** fibula · + Ilizarov lengthening if shortening >2–3 cm (TO) |
| **Fibula** | Resect fibular pseudarthrosis (↑ union) · osteotomise intact fibula |

**Amputation** — after **multiple failed unions**, severe LLD, dysfunctional deformity · **Syme or Boyd** (ankle disarticulation) preferred over amputation at pseudarthrosis

![Vascularised fibular graft](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4056-free-fibula-graft-courtesy-miller-moved.png)

## 6 Complications

**Refracture** — **≥50%** even after union · decreases with age, ceases after maturity

**Other** — **valgus** deformity · **LLD ~5 cm** at maturity · no treatment gives predictable results

## X vs Y

| | **Anterolateral** | **Posteromedial** | **Anteromedial** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Association | **NF1**, fibrous dysplasia | **Calcaneovalgus foot** | **Fibular deficiency** |
| Fracture risk | **High** | Rare | — |
| Treatment | **Brace** until maturity | **Observe** | Per fibular deficiency |
| Sequel | Pseudarthrosis, refracture | **LLD 3–4 cm** | LLD |

## How it's asked

**"All true about CPT EXCEPT?"** → **20% of NF patients have pseudarthrosis** (✓ anterior angulation commonest, ✓ NF commonest association, ✓ dysplastic fibula) (TO)

**"Nonunited tibial fracture + café-au-lait spots — prior deformity?"** → **anterolateral bowing** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: anteromedial (fibular deficiency) · posteromedial (physiologic)

**"Not associated with NF1?"** → **none of the above** (nerve sheath tumour, CPT, scoliosis all associated) (TO)

**"True statement about NF1 and CPT?"** → **6% of NF1 have CPT** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: 50% of NF1 · NF2

**"Anterolateral bowing is associated with?"** → **congenital pseudarthrosis of tibia**

**"5-month-old, anterolateral bow with medullary sclerosis — natural history?"** → **spontaneous fracture**

**"Chromosome 17 mutation, pseudarthrosis after casting — bowing type?"** → **anterolateral**

**"6-month-old posteromedial bow — NOT true?"** → **"pseudarthrosis, protective bracing"** (TO)

**"2-year-old ambulatory, prefracture CPT?"** → **PTB orthosis**\
✗ Wrong options: osteotomy + rod (nonunion)

**"12-year-old NF, anterolateral bow, intact cortices?"** → **AFO with anterior shell** (brace to maturity)

**"Anterolateral bow with cysts, no fracture — ideal surgery?"** → **growth modulation (8-plate)** (TO)

**"Indication for surgery in CPT?"** → **pseudarthrosis (or fracture)** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: bowing >45° · brace failure

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Arthrogryposis

*Pediatrics · 6 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Definition & epidemiology

**Definition** — **non-progressive** congenital disorder · **multiple rigid joints** in all 4 limbs · a physical finding in ~300 disorders (TO)

**Incidence** — **1:3000** live births (TO)

**Course** — contractures **do not worsen with growth**\
✗ Wrong options: "progressive, peak at maturity"

### Etiology

**Mechanism** — **fetal akinesia** (↓ movement in utero) → **symmetric** contractures · movement needed from **8 weeks' gestation** (TO)

**Neurogenic vs myopathic** — **neurogenic 90%**, myopathic 10% (Orthobullets) · neurologic **70–80% / 80%** (TO)

**Pathophysiology** — maternal antibodies vs fetal **acetylcholine receptors** → ↓ anterior horn cells · anterior horn cell disease (Werdnig-Hoffmann, SMA)

**Other causes (TO)** — connective tissue (diastrophic dysplasia, Larsen, Beals) · myopathies · uterine space limits (oligohydramnios, fibroids, multiple pregnancy) · vascular compromise · maternal disease (diabetes, MS, myasthenia, myotonic dystrophy, rubella, CMV, toxoplasma)

**No genetic predisposition** for classic form (OB)

### Classification

| Type | Description |
|---|---|
| **I** | Single localised deformity (e.g. forearm pronation) |
| **II** | **Full expression** — absent shoulder muscles, thin limbs, elbows extended, wrists flexed + ulnar deviated, intrinsic-plus hands, adducted thumbs, **no flexion creases** |
| **III** | Type II + **polydactyly** + non-neuromuscular systems |

**By neurological function (TO)** — normal: **amyoplasia**, distal arthrogryposis, fetal crowding, systemic tissue disorder, multiple pterygium · abnormal neurological function

**Amyoplasia** — **most common recognisable form** · **sporadic**, symmetric · normal/above-average IQ · **midline facial hemangioma** · hip involvement ~**80%**

### Distal arthrogryposis & related syndromes (TO)

**Distal arthrogryposis** — hands + feet, large joints spared · ulnar-deviated fingers at MCP · "cup-like" palm, single crease · flexed adducted thumb · equinovarus or vertical talus · **better prognosis**

**Freeman-Sheldon** — **common variant of type 2 distal arthrogryposis** · **whistling face** (pursed lips, deep eyes)\
✗ Wrong options: Larsen · Escobar

**Pterygium syndromes** — skin webs across joints · **Escobar** = multiple pterygium (neck webs, vertebral anomalies) · **popliteal pterygium** variant

**Larsen** — **filamin B** · hypermobility · **congenital dislocation of multiple joints** (anterior knee dislocation) · **cervical kyphosis/injury** · normal IQ

**Bruck syndrome** — **OI + congenital contractures**

## 3 Presentation

### Clinical characteristics

| Region | Classic finding |
|---|---|
| **Shoulder** | **Adducted, internally rotated** (absent deltoid) |
| **Elbow** | **Extended**, no flexion creases (deficient biceps, brachialis) · rarely flexed |
| **Wrist** | **Flexed + ulnar deviated** (TO) |
| **Hand** | **Intrinsic plus** · IP flexion + relative MCP extension · **adducted thumb / thumb-in-palm** |
| **Hip** | **Flexed, abducted, externally rotated** · subluxation / **teratologic dislocation** |
| **Knee** | Extended (classic) but **most often flexed** · weak quads · dimple over patella |
| **Foot** | **Clubfoot (most common deformity)** · vertical talus rarer |
| **Spine** | **C-shaped neuromuscular** scoliosis — **33%** (Orthobullets) · 28–67% / 10–30% (TO) · long thoracolumbar curves |

**Normal** — **intelligence**, facies, **sensation**, viscera

**Other** — **fractures 25%** · limbs cylindrical/fusiform with ↓ skin creases · **nonambulatory 25%**

![Classic arthrogryposis — IR shoulders, extended elbows, flexed wrists](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4088-arthrogry.jpg)

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Diagnosis & upper-limb pattern

**Diagnosis** — **clinical**: multiple symmetric contractures (e.g. stiff fingers with few creases, IR arms, flexed-ER hips, stiff clubfeet) → **arthrogryposis**\
✗ Wrong options: Larsen · CP · Down

**Studies at 3–4 months** — neurologic studies · enzyme tests · **muscle biopsy** · genetic testing

**Prenatal US (TO)** — lack of movement, fixed flexion, abnormal position, ↑ nuchal translucency, polyhydramnios-type signs, thin ribs, diaphyseal fractures

## 5 Management

**Principle** — multidisciplinary · **stretching + casting first** → soft-tissue release / osteotomy · correct alignment, preserve ROM, tendon transfers for active motion

### Operative criteria

| Region | Indication / timing |
|---|---|
| **Upper limb surgery** | After age **4** (independent eating) (Orthobullets) · after ambulation, **3–4 yr**; not >8 yr (TO) |
| **Elbow extension contracture** | **Triceps V-Y lengthening + posterior capsulectomy at 1.5–3 yr** |
| **Unilateral hip dislocation** | **Open reduction** ≥6 mo (Orthobullets) · **6–9 mo** (OB) · **before 1 yr** (OB) |
| **Knee flexion contracture** | **>30°** → hamstring release at **6–9 mo** (Orthobullets) · not corrected by 6–12 mo (TO) |
| **Hip flexion contracture** | **>45°** → surgical release (TO) |
| **Knee extension contracture** | Failed casting by **6–12 mo** → **quadricepsplasty** (TO) |
| **Foot** | Around time of standing (↓ recurrence) (TO) |

### Upper limb – passive manipulation & casting

**Goals** — drive electric chair, use computer devices · **one elbow extended** (positioning, **perianal care**) + **one flexed** (**feeding**)

**First line** — **passive manipulation + serial casting** (13-month-old, good triceps)\
✗ Wrong options: observation · EMG/NCS · osteotomy · soft-tissue release + transfer

| Deformity | Procedure |
|---|---|
| **Elbow extension** | **Triceps V-Y lengthening + posterior capsulectomy** (passive flexion) · active flexion: **bipolar pectoralis major / latissimus transfer**, long-head triceps transfer, **Steindler flexorplasty** |
| Stiff flexed elbow | Not severe → **no surgery** (TO) |
| **Wrist palmar flexion + ulnar deviation** | **FCU release/lengthening/transfer to wrist extensors** · **dorsal carpal closing-wedge osteotomy** · fusion = salvage · ideal 5–20° dorsiflexion |
| Thumb-in-palm, syndactyly | **Z-plasty**, adductor release, syndactyly release |
| Finger deformity | **PIP arthrodesis** |
| Shoulder IR | External rotation humeral osteotomy (TO) |

### Hip dislocation – open reduction (not Pavlik)

**Teratologic hip** — **68–80%** · dislocates **early in utero** → stiff, rigid

**Pavlik / abduction brace** — **unlikely to succeed** → US still shows **dislocation (α ~40°, β unmeasurable)** · Pavlik in dislocated hip prevents posterior wall development

**Closed reduction** — **rarely successful** (skin/skeletal traction not helpful)

```
Arthrogrypotic hip dislocation
├─ Unilateral → open reduction (prevent pelvic obliquity, scoliosis)
│      ├─ <1 yr (6–9 mo) → medial approach ± femoral shortening
│      └─ Older child → anterior approach + femoral shortening ± pelvic osteotomy
└─ Bilateral → observe (controversial; many ambulate)
       └─ Good arc of motion (e.g. 80°) → bilateral medial open reduction (OB)
```

![Teratologic hip dislocation in arthrogryposis](../../MCQ/attachments/q5255-illa-1305479242-680x400.jpg)

**Approach** — **medial** (good results 80%; Szoke) · **anteromedial or anterolateral** both good (OB) · anterior reserved for **older children**

**Prerequisite** — reasonable flexion/extension arc + active lower-limb movement

**Risk** — resulting **hip flexion contracture worsens gait**

**Sequence conflict** — knee releases **before** hip reduction to help maintain reduction (Orthobullets; TO: correct knees before hip) · **"hip first, knee second"** (other TO explanation) — read the options

### Knee contractures

**Flexion contracture** — serial splinting/casting → **hamstring release** (>30°, early) · ambulation possible with residual **15–20°** · **supracondylar femoral extension osteotomy** at maturity · guided growth with osteotomies (may not fix poor quads) · Ilizarov for severe

**Extension contracture / dislocation** — early stretching + casting · **open quadriceps tenotomy at 4–6 mo** · late **V-Y quadricepsplasty** · reduce knee dislocation first

### Clubfoot & lower-limb deformity management

**Most common deformity in AMC** — **talipes equinovarus** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: CVT · knee flexion · IP flexion

**Newborn** — start **serial stretching + splinting** immediately (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: wait for weight gain · wait 1 month

**Ponseti** — **effective but more casts** than idiopathic · **high relapse** (27% at 2 yr) (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: as effective · not effective · not used

```
Arthrogrypotic clubfoot
├─ Early → Ponseti casting (more casts)
├─ Rigid / failed Ponseti → posteromedial + posterolateral release
└─ Recurrence after release → talectomy (child) / triple arthrodesis (adolescent)
       (alternatives: Verebelyi-Ogston talar decancellation, lateral column shortening, circular frame)
```

### Congenital vertical talus

**CVT in arthrogryposis** — **soft-tissue release first** · **talectomy** if recurrence

**CVT associations** — **hip dislocation 43–50%** (✓ "congenital hip dislocation") · myelomeningocele, arthrogryposis, **trisomy 18**, Cri-du-Chat · familial **HOXD10** · 60–85% have associated anomalies\
✗ Wrong options: amniotic band · fibular hemimelia · PFFD · polydactyly

**Spine** — multilevel fusion for arthrogrypotic curves

## X vs Y

| | **Arthrogryposis (amyoplasia)** | **Larsen** | **Distal arthrogryposis** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Joints | **Stiff, rigid**, all 4 limbs | **Hypermobile**, multiple **dislocations** | Hands + feet; large joints spared |
| Gene | Sporadic | **Filamin B** | Variants incl. Freeman-Sheldon |
| Hallmark | IR shoulders, extended elbows, flexed-ulnar wrists | Anterior knee dislocation, **cervical kyphosis** | Ulnar-deviated fingers, cup palm |
| IQ | Normal | Normal | Normal |

## How it's asked

**"All characteristic of arthrogryposis EXCEPT?"** → **progressive contractures peaking at maturity**

**"Arthrogrypotic newborn, Pavlik 4 weeks — US at 1 month?"** → **α 40°, β unmeasurable** (still dislocated)

**"Amyoplasia, unilateral irreducible hip?"** → **delayed open reduction ± pelvic/femoral osteotomy** (6–9 mo)\
✗ Wrong options: Pavlik · semi-rigid brace · traction · closed reduction + spica

**"10-month-old arthrogrypotic, bilateral dislocations, 80° arc?"** → **bilateral open reduction, medial approach**\
✗ Wrong options: traction + CR · anterior approach

**"6-month-old, rigid unilateral dislocation — initial management?"** → **open reduction (anteromedial/anterolateral)**\
✗ Wrong options: Pavlik · observe · wait >1 yr · spica

**"13-month-old, little elbow motion, good triceps?"** → **passive manipulation + serial casting**

**"Rigid rocker-bottom foot — associated condition?"** → **congenital hip dislocation**

**"Stiff fingers, IR arms, flexed-ER hips, stiff clubfeet — diagnosis?"** → **arthrogryposis** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: Larsen · CP · Down

**"Common variant of distal arthrogryposis?"** → **Freeman-Sheldon** (TO)

**"Classical arthrogryposis commonly involves?"** → **wrist flexed with ulnar deviation** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: thumb abduction · flexed elbows · finger extension

**"Most common deformity in AMC?"** → **talipes equinovarus** (TO)

**"10-day-old with AMC — advice for lower limbs?"** → **serial stretching and splinting** (TO)

**"Ponseti in arthrogrypotic clubfoot?"** → **effective but more casts needed** (TO)

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Beckwith-Wiedemann Syndrome

*Pediatrics · 7 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **1 in 13,700** births · M:F **1:1**

**Type** — **overgrowth syndrome**

### Genes & lab markers

**Locus** — **chromosome 11p15.5**

**Mechanism** — overactivity of **IGF-2** (growth factor) and/or loss of active **CDKN1C** (cell-proliferation inhibitor)

**Inheritance** — **sporadic 85%** · **AD with incomplete penetrance 15%**

**Lab** — **neonatal hypoglycemia** (✗ Wrong options: hyperglycemia) from **pancreatic islet cell hypertrophy** (✗ Wrong options: pancreatic insufficiency) → repeated bouts → **spasticity**, altered development

## 3 Presentation

### Diagnostic criteria

| Major | Minor |
|---|---|
| **Overgrowth** (large for gestational age, gigantism) | **Hemihypertrophy** — **10–20%** (Orthobullets) · **~15%** (OB) |
| **Abdominal wall defects** incl. **omphalocele** | **Ear anomalies** (linear lobe creases) |
| **Macroglossia** | **Neonatal hypoglycemia** |
| | **Nephromegaly** |

**Exam** — macroglossia · midface hypoplasia · infraorbital creases · anterior linear ear-lobe creases · **visceromegaly** · exophthalmos · **hemihypertrophy** (whole side incl. foot)

![Beckwith-Wiedemann — macroglossia](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4110-bechwith-wiedemann-syndrome-300x228.jpg)

**No skin markings** — absence of nevi / vascular markings excludes **NF, Proteus, Klippel-Trenaunay** as causes of hemihypertrophy

![Hemihypertrophy in Beckwith-Wiedemann](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4110-hemihypertrophy.jpg)

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

**Diagnosis** — **physical exam + genetic testing**

### Key radiology findings

**Radiographs** — **comparison view** of affected vs contralateral limb (LLD)

**Bone age** — inaccurate when very young · later useful to **time epiphysiodesis**\
✗ Wrong options: as initial study at age 2

## 5 Management

### Wilms tumour screening

**Predisposition** — **embryonal tumours**: **Wilms tumour** (most common), **hepatoblastoma**, adrenal neoplasm · hemihypertrophy from BWS → **40% malignancy risk** (OB)

**Screening** — **renal + abdominal ultrasound every 3–6 months until 5–8 years** (Orthobullets) · until **~7 years** (OB) · intervals ≤4 months ↓ late-stage disease

**Tumour side** — may be **contralateral** to hemihypertrophy

```
Child with hemihypertrophy (BWS)
├─ Screening → abdominal/renal US q3–6 mo until 5–8 yr (Wilms)
└─ LLD → serial limb radiographs, bone age later
       └─ Predicted significant LLD → epiphysiodesis of affected (longer) limb
```

### Operative criteria

**Epiphysiodesis** — **growth arrest of the affected (overgrown) limb** for LLD

**Multidisciplinary** — orthopaedic, abdominal and overgrowth issues

## How it's asked

**"Large-for-dates neonate with hypoglycemia, now right hemihypertrophy + 2 cm LLD — next imaging?"** → **renal and abdominal ultrasonography**\
✗ Wrong options: left-wrist bone age · MRI spine/brain · hip US

**"11p15.5 mutation, hemihypertrophy, macroglossia, omphalocele — screen every 3–6 months for?"** → **Wilms tumor**\
✗ Wrong options: hearing loss · hyperglycemia · pancreatic insufficiency · vision loss

**"Cause of infantile hypoglycemia in BWS?"** → **pancreatic islet cell hypertrophy**

**"Treatment of BWS limb overgrowth?"** → **epiphysiodesis** of affected limb

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Bipartite Patella

*Pediatrics · 8 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **2–3%** (Orthobullets) · **~2%** (TO) · "1 in 20" (OB)

**Sex** — **no sex predilection** (Orthobullets) · **M:F 9:1** (TO) — read the options

**Bilateral** — **50%** (Orthobullets, OB) · "most patients" (TO)

**Location** — **superolateral (Saupe type III)** most common

### Etiology & anatomy

**Nature** — **normal variant**, **failure of fusion** of accessory ossification centre · often confused with **patella fracture**

**Ossification** — patella = **largest sesamoid**, single nucleus · ossifies 4–5 yr ♂ / 3 yr ♀ · **accessory centre at 8–12 yr** · fragment joined by **fibrocartilage**

**Painful bipartite patella** — trauma disrupts **fibrocartilaginous zone** → cannot unite (no arterial penetration to fragment) · **vastus lateralis traction** → separation/nonunion

**Blood supply** — predominantly **distal → proximal** · 6 arteries: superior + inferior medial/lateral geniculates (popliteal), supreme geniculate (SFA), anterior tibial recurrent

**Associated** — **nail-patella syndrome**

### Histology

**Interposed tissue** — **fibrocartilage** > fibrous > hyaline · **avascular**

**Adjacent bone** — scalloped, osteoclasts, **well vascularised**

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — mostly **asymptomatic, incidental** · only **2% symptomatic**: anterior knee pain after direct trauma (fall, kick) or overuse (cycling, hill climbing) · worse squatting, jumping, stairs · giving way

**Exam** — tenderness over fragment (superolateral) · hematoma · quad inhibition · palpable defect / large patella

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

**AP knee** — **best view** to see bipartite patella

**Skyline** — prone (non-weight-bearing) vs **squatting (weight-bearing)** → ↑ **separation** of fragments on squatting view

**Contralateral knee** — similar findings in 50%

**Bipartite vs fracture** — **superolateral** · **smooth, rounded borders** · often bilateral

**MRI** — painful bipartite patella → **edema** around fragment = pain source

**Bone scan** — equivocal X-ray: ↑ **uptake superolateral** · controversial

**CT** — shows fragment, **not edema**

![Bipartite patella type III — AP](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4049-bipartite-type-iii-xr.jpg)

### Saupe classification

| Type | Incidence | Location | Image |
|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | 5% | **Inferior pole** (transverse split) | ![Type I](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4049-type-1.jpg) |
| **II** | 20% | **Lateral margin** (longitudinal) | ![Type II](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4049-type-2.jpg) |
| **III** | **75%** | **Superolateral pole** (oblique) | ![Type III](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4049-type-3.jpg) |

## 5 Management

```
Bipartite patella
├─ Asymptomatic / benign exam → reassure, symptomatic Rx, RTP as tolerated
├─ Symptomatic → rest, NSAIDs, isometric quads, brace 30° flexion, ± steroid ≥6 months
└─ Failed >6 mo / displaced / significant impairment → excision of fragment
       └─ Superolateral fragment → ± lateral release or subperiosteal VL release
```

### Location & nonoperative care

**Incidental with benign exam** — **reassurance + observation** · **symptomatic treatment, return to play as tolerated**\
✗ Wrong options: MRI · ORIF · cast 6 wk · immobilise 120° (that's quad contusion)

**Nonop ≥6 months** — rest/sport restriction · NSAIDs · **isometric quadriceps** strengthening · **brace in 30° flexion** · local corticosteroid · less successful in young athletes (compliance)

### Operative criteria

| Indication for surgery |
|---|
| Failed nonop **>6 months** |
| **Displaced fragment** needing reduction |
| Direct trauma causing onset of pain |
| Significant impairment of daily activities |

| Procedure | Note |
|---|---|
| **Open excision** | **Most common**, good results · poor if **large articular** fragment (PF incongruity) |
| Arthroscopic excision | Faster recovery, spares quad tendon |
| **Lateral retinacular release** | Superolateral fragment — removes **VL traction** |
| **Subperiosteal VL release** | Superolateral; avoids long lateral release; arthroscopic = quicker, less effusion |
| ORIF | **Large fragments** · controversial |

## 6 Complications

**PF maltracking** — excision of large fragment / lateral release → PF degeneration

**Others** — **effusion** (arthrocentesis) · persistent pain (after lateral release) · quad weakness · osteonecrosis

### Differential – dorsal defect of patella; double-layered patella (MED)

**Double-layered patella** — **pathognomonic of MED** · two segments split by **coronal septum** · anterior in quad/patellar tendons · posterior articulates · painful snapping, locking, dislocation → **fusion** of segments (best) or resection (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: CDP · SED · achondroplasia

**Dorsal defect of patella** — round, lytic, well-defined **subchondral** lesion, **superolateral** quadrant, sclerotic rim, no matrix, no periosteal reaction · MRI: T2 bright, no marrow edema · usually incidental, **self-limited** → activity modification · rarely curettage + graft (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: OCD · chondroblastoma · Brodie abscess

## X vs Y

| | **Bipartite patella** | **Patella fracture** | **Dorsal defect of patella** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Site | **Superolateral** | Any | Superolateral, **subchondral dorsal** |
| Edges | **Smooth, rounded** | Sharp | Sclerotic rim, lytic |
| Bilateral | 50% | No | — |
| Treatment | Symptomatic | Per fracture | Self-limited |

## How it's asked

**"Knee pain after fall, benign exam — which X-ray warrants reassurance?"** → **bipartite patella**\
✗ Wrong options: patellar dislocation · patella alta/tendon rupture · Segond · GCT

**"Helmet to knee, benign exam, bipartite patella on X-ray — next?"** → **symptomatic treatment, RTP as tolerated**\
✗ Wrong options: MRI · ORIF

**"Most common location?"** → **superolateral** (Saupe III)

**"Double-layered patella is pathognomonic of?"** → **MED** (TO)

**"12-year-old, superolateral dorsal subchondral lucency with sclerotic rim?"** → **dorsal defect of patella** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: OCD · chondroblastoma · Brodie abscess

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Bladder Exstrophy

*Pediatrics · 9 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Classic exstrophy** — **1/40,000**

**Cloacal exstrophy** — **1/200,000** (intestinal tract also involved)

### Etiology

**Pathology** — abnormal anterior rupture of the **cloacal membrane** early in embryonic period · inhibited mesenchymal ingrowth into abdominal wall · **altered sclerotome migration** to anterior elements (pubis)

**Result** — failure of abdominal wall closure → **posterior bladder wall protrudes** through lower abdomen

## 3 Presentation

**GU** — **exposed bladder** · urinary tract infections · epispadias (males)

**Musculoskeletal** — acetabula **~12° retroverted** (no pubis to tether anterior ring → posterior elements retrovert) · **waddling gait** with **external foot progression**

![Bladder exstrophy — exposed bladder](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4125-bladder-clinical.jpg)

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

**AP pelvis** — **pubic rami diastasis** · **shortened pubic rami** · **acetabular retroversion**

### Acetabular retroversion

**Most common acetabular finding** — **retroversion**\
✗ Wrong options: anteversion · protrusio · neutral · hypoplasia

![Pubic diastasis in bladder exstrophy](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob4125-bladders-exs-xr.jpg)

## 5 Management

**Goals** — close abdominal wall · **urinary continence** · normal renal function · functional genitalia · reconstruction depends on a **stable anterior pelvic ring**

**All cases** — **surgery**, staged and multidisciplinary (urology, general surgery)

| Stage | Procedure | Age |
|---|---|---|
| **I** | **Primary bladder closure** | Newborn |
| **II** | **Epispadias repair** (males) | 1–2 yr |
| **III** | **Bladder neck reconstruction** | 4 yr |
| Any stage | **Pelvic osteotomies** — ↓ tension on bladder + abdominal wall → ↓ **dehiscence** | — |

```
Pelvic osteotomy fixation by age
├─ Newborn → none (skin traction, hips flexed 90°)
├─ Young child → external fixation
└─ Age >8 → plate fixation to augment diastasis correction
```

## 6 Complications

**Recurrent pubic diastasis** — common **with or without osteotomy** · does not affect activity level

**Anterior innominate osteotomy** — wound dehiscence · **transient femoral nerve palsy**

## How it's asked

**"Most common acetabular finding in bladder exstrophy?"** → **retroversion**

**"Pelvic radiograph findings?"** → **pubic diastasis, short pubic rami, acetabular retroversion**

**"Why pelvic osteotomy?"** → **↓ tension on bladder/abdominal closure → ↓ dehiscence**

**"Gait in bladder exstrophy?"** → **waddling, external foot progression**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Both Bone Forearm Fracture - Pediatric

*Pediatrics · 10 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **~40% of all pediatric fractures** · males > females

**Location** — distal physis 14% · **distal metaphysis 60%** · midshaft 20% · proximal third 4%

**Peak** — during peak bone turnover (remodelling mismatch)

**Associated** — **floating elbow** (ipsilateral supracondylar) **15%** · nerve injury **1%**, most often **median**

### Anatomy

**Bowing** — physiologic **apex-lateral radial bow** · apex-posterior ulnar bow

**Deforming forces** — **biceps + supinator** flex/supinate proximal fragment · **pronator teres + quadratus** pronate distal fragment · **brachioradialis** dorsiflexes + radially deviates distal fragment

**Periosteum** — intact on **concave** side · interosseous membrane taut in **neutral to slight supination**

## 2 Mechanism

**Mechanism** — fall from height, sport, **playground (monkey bars)**

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — forearm pain, refuses to use arm

**Exam** — swelling, deformity, ecchymosis · **subtle poke-hole open wounds** · check elbow + wrist · NV exam · **compartment syndrome**

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

**Types** — **incomplete**: greenstick, torus, **plastic deformation** · **complete** · proximal / middle / distal third · apex volar or dorsal

### Assessing rotational alignment

| View | Landmarks **180° apart** when aligned |
|---|---|
| **AP** | **Radial styloid + bicipital tuberosity** |
| **Lateral** | **Ulnar styloid + coronoid** |

**Other clues** — fragment **diameters** and **cortical thickness** should match

![Rotational alignment — bicipital tuberosity vs radial styloid](../../MCQ/attachments/q3427-illa-bbfa5.jpg)

### Key radiology findings

**Views** — **AP + lateral forearm** + orthogonal **elbow and wrist**

**Findings** — both bones fractured · one fracture + **plastic deformation** of the other · atypical bowing without fracture = plastic deformation

## 5 Management

### Acceptable alignment & remodelling by age

| Age | Angulation | Malrotation | Bayonet apposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| **<10 yr** | **<15°** | **<45°** | **Yes** if <1 cm short (100% translation OK) |
| **≥10 yr** | **<10°** | **<30°** | **No** |
| **<2 yr growth left** | **0°** | **0°** | **No** — adult tolerances |

**By level (girls ≤8, boys ≤10)** — **distal third 20° · middle 15° · proximal 10°** · remodelling ↓ distal → proximal

**Bayonet** — acceptable (esp. distal radius) if angulation ≤20° and ≥2 yr growth remain

**8-year-old, 12° dorsal angulation at 1 week** → acceptable → **no sequelae**\
✗ Wrong options: ↓ rotation · malunion · nonunion

### Operative criteria

| Indication | Treatment |
|---|---|
| **Unacceptable alignment** after CR (beyond table) | Fixation |
| Bayonet apposition **>10 yr** | Fixation |
| Both-bone fracture **<13 yr** | **Elastic IM nails** |
| Both-bone fracture **>13 yr** (near maturity) | **ORIF (plate)** |
| **Open fracture**, **refracture** | ORIF |
| Comminuted / segmental, highly displaced | ORIF (relative) |
| **Malunion with callus** + lost rotation (missed follow-up) | **ORIF** (open callus removal) (✗ Wrong options: brace · recast · repeat CR · cast wedging) |

```
Pediatric both-bone forearm fracture
├─ Acceptable after CR → cast, weekly X-rays
└─ Unacceptable / lost reduction
       ├─ <13 yr, simple midshaft → elastic IM nails
       └─ >13 yr, comminuted, open, refracture → ORIF
```

### Casting technique – cast index & loss of reduction

**Closed reduction** — traction (finger traps), thumb pressure, rotation, **3-point bending** · **apex volar → pronate** · **apex dorsal → supinate**

**Plastic deformation** — accept if **≤20°** · reduce with **slow steady 3-point bending** (abrupt force → fracture)

**Short vs long arm cast** (distal BBFF) — **no difference** in loss of reduction · SAC fewer complications\
✗ Wrong options: more swelling, pain, remanipulation

**Cast index** — **lateral inner width ÷ AP inner width** · **>0.8 → loss of reduction**\
✗ Wrong options: pain · physeal arrest · nonunion · swelling

![Cast index](../../MCQ/attachments/q4865-illa-cast-index.jpg)

**Remanipulation risk** — **initial fracture displacement** (angulation + translation) · same-level radius + ulna fractures · cast index >0.8 · residual deformity\
✗ Wrong options: mechanism · distal location · below-elbow cast · cast index <0.7

**Swelling** — bivalve cast (compartment syndrome)

### Operative fixation – intramedullary nails vs plates

**Ulnar nail** — **olecranon tip or through anconeus** (protect ulnar nerve)

**Radial nail** — just proximal to **radial styloid** or dorsal distal radius proximal to physis · dorsal central start risks **EPL**

**Technique** — reduce easiest bone first · **open the fracture after 2–3 failed passes** · nail removal at 3–4 months

| | **Elastic IM nails** | **ORIF plate** |
|---|---|---|
| Time | **Shorter** | Longer |
| Blood loss | **Less** | More |
| Union, **radial bow**, rotation | **Equal** | Equal |
| Refracture | **Lower** (no screw holes) | Higher after plate removal |
| Best for | Midshaft, transverse/oblique, minimal comminution, **acute** (pre-callus) | Comminution, older, open |

## 6 Complications

### Complication – loss of forearm rotation

**Most common long-term after IM fixation** — **loss of pronation/supination** (soft-tissue injury, stripping)\
✗ Wrong options: infection · malunion · refracture · nonunion

**Refracture** — **5–10%** · risks: **plate removal**, **greenstick** pattern → **ORIF**

**Malunion** — loss of rotation · from initial reduction or delayed diagnosis → **corrective osteotomy**

**Compartment syndrome** — high energy · **multiple reduction / nail-passage attempts** → **fasciotomy**

**Synostosis** — rare · head injury, high energy · resection rarely helps

## How it's asked

**"14-year-old both-bone fracture — acceptable angulation, malrotation, bayonet?"** → **0°, 0°, none**

**"8-year-old, 12° angulation after loss of reduction — outcome?"** → **no additional sequelae**

**"7-year-old acceptable alignment at 1 week?"** → **observation**\
✗ Wrong options: repeat CR · ORIF · IMN

**"Short vs long arm cast after anatomic CR of distal BBFF?"** → **no difference in outcomes**

**"Cast index 0.95 — risk?"** → **loss of reduction**

**"Factor predisposing to remanipulation?"** → **initial fracture displacement**

**"IMN vs plate — true statement?"** → **radial bow restoration similar**\
✗ Wrong options: IMN higher nonunion · more blood loss · longer surgery

**"Missed follow-up, 6 weeks, healed malaligned, lost pronosupination?"** → **ORIF radius + ulna**\
✗ Wrong options: cast wedging · repeat CR

**"Radiographic rotational alignment?"** → **AP: radial styloid + biceps tuberosity 180° apart**

**"Most common long-term complication after IM fixation?"** → **loss of forearm rotation**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Brachymetatarsia

*Pediatrics · 11 / 112*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Sex** — **female:male 25:1**

**Location** — **4th metatarsal** most common · **often bilateral**

### Etiology
